A Generalization of Carter–Payne Homomorphisms
Martín Forsberg Conde

Abstract
. We construct graded homomorphisms between Specht modules of quiver Hecke algebras of type A that differ by an “ e e -small” partition-shaped removable set of nodes by expanding on methods by Lyle and Mathas. Our main result constitutes a full generalization of the classical result by Carter and Payne for Specht modules of the symmetric group.



1.
Introduction

This paper is concerned with the problem of finding homomorphisms between Specht modules of cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras of type A. We build upon methods developed by Lyle and Mathas
[
13
]
.

Carter and Lusztig
[
3
]
gave an important family of homomorphisms between Weyl modules of the general linear groups whose respective partitions differ by a single row-strip of nodes under certain conditions in positive characteristic
p
. Through the Schur functor, these maps can be seen as homomorphisms between Specht modules of the symmetric group. This result was later extended by Carter and Payne to the following theorem, remarkable for its generality and simplicity.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
4
]
)

Suppose
\lambda,\mu
are partitions of
n
such that the Young diagram for
\mu
is obtained from that of
\lambda
by raising
d
nodes from row
j
to row
i
. Suppose that
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+j-i+d
is divisible by
p(d)
, the smallest power of
p
such that
p(d)>d
. Then
\Hom_{k\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(S^{\lambda},S^{\mu})\neq 0
.

Cyclotomic Hecke algebras of type A generalize the group algebra of the symmetric group. They are known to be Morita equivalent to a direct sum of tensor products of
integral
cyclotomic Hecke algebras of type A
[
7
]
. Moreover, all integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras of type A over a field are isomorphic to quiver Hecke algebras according to the celebrated isomorphism theorem of Brundan and Kleshchev
[
1
]
.

The representation theory of integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras and cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras depends crucially on a parameter
e
, called the
quantum characteristic
of the algebra. For the group algebra of the symmetric group, this parameter coincides with
p
, the characteristic of the ground field.

Lyle and Mathas
[
13
]
gave a partial generalization of
1.1
to cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras. Furthermore, their methods allowed them to prove that the homomorphisms they find are homogeneous of positive degree. The generalization is only partial because Lyle and Mathas’s result requires that the removable strip of nodes has length
d<e
. In contrast, the classical Carter–Payne theorem allows for longer strips to be moved, under stronger divisibility conditions. It should be noted that further generalizations are described in
[
13
]
, but we do not discuss them in this text.

A shape
[\xi]\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
is an orthogonally connected subset of
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
, the Young diagram of the multipartition
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. The shape
[\xi]
is said to be removable if
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\setminus[\xi]
is the Young diagram of a multipartition. The shape
[\xi]
is said to be
straight
if its nodes are in the shape of the Young diagram of a partition. It is said to be
skew
if its nodes are in the shape of the Young diagram of a skew partition. See
section

2.1
for the background and notation on partitions and tableaux that we use.

In his doctoral thesis, Witty
[
19
, Theorem 3.14]
gave an important generalization of Lyle and Mathas’s result in the case of bipartitions with zero quantum characteristic: according to his result, there exist homomorphisms between bipartitions differing by a removable skew shape (instead of a removable row-strip). The techniques in this paper can be used to give a new proof of Witty’s result. We comment on this at the end of
section

6
.

The main theorems in
[
4
]
,
[
13
]
and
[
19
]
hold under very different conditions. This paper aims to unify and substantially generalize
[
13
]
and
[
4
]
, and in doing so we make significant progress into further unifying these results with
[
19
, Theorem 3.14]
. Speaking concretely, a simple result in
section

4
extends
[
13
, Theorem 3.12]
into a full generalization of
1.1
; and
1.2
, our main result, further unifies
[
13
, Theorem 3.13]
and
1.1
with the special case in
[
19
, Theorem 3.14]
where the two components differ by a
straight
shape. The author expects that a full unification of the three results holds, see
6.11
. We also refine the methods in
[
13
]
to give an explicit description of the homomorphisms in the main theorem, which send the original Specht module generator to a single standard basis element of the new Specht module.

It is significant that
1.2
gives many new homomorphisms even in the special case where the quiver Hecke algebra is isomorphic to the symmetric group. Our theorem constitutes the second generalization of
1.1
that produces new homomorphisms between Specht modules of the symmetric group, after
[
9
]
. The new homomorphisms given in this paper never coincide with those given in
[
9
]
. Before conjecturing a unification of
1.2
with the main result of
[
9
]
, a description of the latter in the quiver Hecke algebra setting would be desirable.

Next we quickly skim over the definitions we need to state the main theorem. We explain the necessary background more leisurely in
section

2
. Let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}
. An
\ell
-multicharge
is an
\ell
-tuple of elements of
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, denoted by
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell})
. Given
e
,
\kappa
and an
\ell
-multipartition
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash_{\ell}n
, the
residue
of a node
N=(r,c,m)\in[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
is
\res(N)=c-r+\kappa_{m}\mod e
. A shape
[\xi]
is said to be
e
-small
if its nodes do not contain the full set
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
as residues. We order the nodes of
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
in lexicographic order according to their component, row and column, in that order of precedence.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
be multipartitions of
n
such that the Young diagram of
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can be constructed from
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
by removing a removable
e
-small straight shape and placing it at an earlier position in the multipartition so that the residues match. Then there is a homogeneous homomorphism from
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to
S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
of positive degree which maps the Specht module generator of
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to a single standard basis element of
S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

The standard basis of a Specht module depends on a certain choice of reduced words for permutations. The theorem above holds for a specific choice of reduced words, which we describe in
section

2
, along with the necessary background on partitions, cyclotomic Hecke algebras and cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras.

In
section

3
, we introduce accessible nodes and generalize stubborn strings as introduced in
[
13
]
. The tools introduced in this section are essential to the proof of the main theorem.

In
section

4
, we use the technique of change of rings to reduce homomorphisms between Specht modules for integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras with
e=r^{a},p=0
, where
r\in\mathbb{N}
is prime, to homomorphisms between Specht modules for integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras with
e=p=r
.

In
section

5
we go over the result by Lyle and Mathas where the partitions differ by a single (
e
-small) row-strip. The proof given here differs slightly from that in
[
13
]
so that it more closely resembles the proof of the more general
6.1
. We hope that the inclusion of this section will improve the readability of the text.

In
section

6
we state the Main
6.1
, providing information about the degree of the maps as well as an explicit description of the homomorphisms. Most of the section is dedicated to the proof of this theorem. We finish with a conjecture about Carter–Payne homomorphisms between Specht modules whose multipartitions differ by a skew shape.

2.
Background

2.1.
Partitions and tableaux

A
partition

\lambda
of
n
is a weakly decreasing sequence of natural numbers
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{k})
which sum to
n
. We write
|\lambda|=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}=n
. When useful, we take partitions to be an infinite sequence where
\lambda_{k}
is followed by infinitely many trailing zeros. An
\ell
-multipartition
of
n
is a sequence of partitions
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{\ell})
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|\lambda^{j}|=n
. Each
\lambda^{j}
inside a multipartition is called a
component
. We denote multipartitions with boldface greek letters, and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash_{\ell}n
denotes that
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is an
\ell
-multipartition of
n
. The set of
\ell
-multipartitions of
n
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}_{n}
. We sometimes write simply
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash n
. Given a multipartition
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, its
Young diagram
is the set
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]=\{(r,c,m)|c\leq\lambda^{m}_{r}\}
. Young diagrams are to be thought of graphically: for instance, the Young diagram for
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\left((5,3),(2),(3,3)\right)
is given by

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

The
dominance order
on multipartitions is denoted by
\trianglelefteq
. That is, we write
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\trianglelefteq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
if, for all
m^{*}\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and
r^{*}\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{m^{*}-1}|\lambda^{i}|+\sum_{j=1}^{r^{*}}|\lambda^{m^{*}}_{j}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m^{*}-1}|\mu^{i}|+\sum_{j=1}^{r^{*}}|\mu^{m^{*}}_{j}|
.

If a node in a Young diagram can be removed and the remaining nodes still form the Young diagram for some multipartition, the node is said to be
removable
. Similarly, if a node can be added to a Young diagram to form the Young diagram for a new multipartition, the node is said to be
addable
.

A
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableau
\mathfrak{t}
is a bijection
\mathfrak{t}{:}[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\rightarrow\{1,\dots,n\}
. Tableaux are usually depicted by writing the associated numbers inside each node of the Young diagram. A
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableau is
standard
if its entries increase along the rows and down the columns within each component. We denote the set of standard
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableaux by
\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
. Given
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash n
and a standard
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableau

\mathfrak{t}
, the restriction
\mathfrak{t}_{\downarrow m}
of
\mathfrak{t}
, for
m<n
, is the tableau given by removing all nodes with numbers
m+1,\dots,n
from the diagram. We define a partial order on
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableaux that we call
dominance order
, obtained by declaring
\mathfrak{t}\succeq\mathfrak{s}
if
\Shape(\mathfrak{t}_{\downarrow m})\trianglerighteq\Shape(\mathfrak{s}_{\downarrow m})
for all
m\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, where
\Shape(\mathfrak{t})
is the multipartition such that
\mathfrak{t}
is a
\Shape(\mathfrak{t})
-tableau. We define the initial tableau
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to be the unique maximal tableau under dominance order. The initial tableau
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
gives a natural order to the nodes of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
: if
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, then we write
N_{1}>N_{2}
if
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(N_{1})>\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(N_{2})
.

Next, let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup{\infty}
, and let
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell})
be an
\ell
-tuple of elements of
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
. Given
e
,
\kappa
and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash_{\ell}n
, the
residue
of a node
N=(r,c,m)\in[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
is defined to be
\res(N)=c-r+\kappa_{m}\pmod{e}
. Given a residue
i\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, the node
N
is said to be an
i
-node if
\res(N)=i
. For instance, for
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=((5,3,2),\varnothing,(3,1))
,
e=3
and
\kappa=(0,0,1)
, the residues associated to the Young diagram are given by

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\right).

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash n
, and let
N\in{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a removable node. We define

d_{N}({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})=\#\{\text{addable $i$-nodes $A\in[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]$ such that $A>N$}\}\\
-\#\{\text{removable $i$-nodes $A\in[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]$ such that $A>N$}\}.

Next we define the
degree
of a tableau
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
recursively: let
N=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(n)
be the node occupied by
n
in
\mathfrak{t}
. Then we have

(1)

\deg(\mathfrak{t})=\deg(\mathfrak{t}_{\downarrow(n-1)})+d_{N}({\boldsymbol{\lambda}}).

This degree function is connected to the grading on
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a multipartition of
n
. A
shape

[\xi]\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
is an orthogonally connected subset of
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
. A shape
[\xi]
is said to be
e
-small
if its nodes do not contain the full set
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
as residues. Moreover, a shape
[\xi]
is said to be
straight
if its nodes are in the shape of the Young diagram of a partition, and it is said to be
skew
if its nodes are in the shape of the Young diagram of a skew partition. A shape
[\xi]
is said to be
removable
if one can reach
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\setminus[\xi]
from
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
by successively substracting removable nodes. Finally, let
[\xi]
and
[\rho]
be straight shapes, and let
\xi
and
\rho
be the partitions associated with the shapes
[\xi]
and
[\rho]
. If
\xi_{j}\leq\rho_{j}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and the residue of the top-left node of
\xi
matches the residue of the top-left node of
\rho
, then we say that
[\xi]
is a
subshape
of
[\rho]
.

Example 2.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=((5,3,2),\varnothing,(3,1))
,
e=3
and
\kappa=(0,0,1)
. Let
[\xi]\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
be the set of shaded nodes inside the first component and let
[\rho]
be the set of shaded nodes inside the third component. Then both are straight shapes, and
[\rho]
is a subshape of
[\xi]
. The shape
[\rho]
is
e
-small, while
[\xi]
is not.

If a node
N\in{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
satisfies
N<N^{\prime}
for all
N^{\prime}\in[\xi]\subset{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
we write
N\prec[\xi]
.
If
N
satisfies
N\geq N^{\prime}
for some
N^{\prime}\in[\xi]
, then we write
N\nprec[\xi]
. Further, given two shapes
[\xi]
and
[\rho]
, we may write
[\xi]\prec[\rho]
if
N\prec[\rho]
for each
N\in[\xi]
.

Given a straight shape or a Young diagram
[\xi]
, a
hook
of
[\xi]
is a
\Gamma
-shaped strip of nodes whose north-eastern and south-western tips are orthogonally connected to the right and bottom boundaries of
[\xi]
respectively, while a
rim hook
of
[\xi]
is a removable shape with at most one node in each diagonal. We picture a hook and a rim hook for the partition
(5,4,4,3)\vdash 16
below.

2.2.
Cyclotomic Hecke algebras

From now on we fix a field
k
of characteristic
p
, possibly zero. Unless stated otherwise, all algebras throughout this text are
k
-algebras. Cyclotomic Hecke algebras are Hecke algebras for the wreath product
C_{\ell}\wr\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, where
C_{\ell}
is the cyclic group with
\ell
elements. This is the complex reflection group of type
G(\ell,1,n)
. In particular, if
\ell=1
, the cyclotomic Hecke algebra is the Hecke algebra of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and for
\ell=2
, it is the Hecke algebra of
C_{2}\wr\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, which is the Coxeter group of type B or C.

Next we give the definition of cyclotomic Hecke algebras with generators and relations, following
[
16
]
. Let
v\in k\setminus\{0\}
and
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{\ell}\in k
. The
cyclotomic Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\ell}(v;Q_{1},\dots,Q_{\ell})
has generators
L_{1},\dots,L_{n},T_{1},\dots,T_{n-1}
and relations

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}(L_{1}-Q_{i})=0,

\displaystyle(T_{r}+v^{-1})(T_{r}-v)=0,

\displaystyle L_{r+1}=T_{r}L_{r}T_{r}+L_{r},

\displaystyle T_{r}T_{s}=T_{s}T_{r}\text{ if }|r-s|\geq 2,

\displaystyle T_{s}T_{s+1}T_{s}=T_{s+1}T_{s}T_{s+1},

\displaystyle T_{r}L_{t}=L_{t}T_{r}\text{ if }t\neq r,r+1.

Given
v\in k\setminus\{0\}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the
quantum integer

[m]_{v}
is defined as
[m]_{v}=v+v^{3}+\dots+v^{2m-1}
. Let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}
. If
e
is finite, let
\zeta\in k
be a primitive
e
-th root of unity, and otherwise let
\zeta\in k
be a non-root of unity. Let
\kappa\in\left(\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\ell}
and define
\Lambda(\kappa)=\Lambda_{\kappa_{1}}+\dots+\Lambda_{\kappa_{\ell}}
, where
\Lambda_{i}
are the fundamental weights as defined in
[
10
]
. We also make use of the normalized invariant bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfying

(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=a_{ij},\qquad(\Lambda_{i},\alpha_{j})=\delta_{ij}.\qquad(i,j\in I)

The
integral
cyclotomic Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
is the cyclotomic Hecke algebra with parameters
v=\zeta
and
Q_{j}=[\kappa_{j}]_{\zeta}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
. In
section

4
, we will use the notation
\mathcal{H}_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(R)
, where
\zeta
is a root of unity, to mean the integral cyclotomic Hecke algebra with
v=\zeta
and
Q_{i}=[\kappa_{j}]_{\zeta}
over a commutative ring
R
. Dipper and Mathas
[
7
]
showed that all cyclotomic Hecke algebras are Morita equivalent to a direct sum of tensor products of integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras.

2.3.
Cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras

Let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup{\infty}
. Given a quiver of affine type
A_{e-1}^{(1)}
, we assign the vertices of our quiver labels
0,\dots,e-1
so that there is an edge
i\rightarrow j
if and only if
j=i-1\pmod{e}
. If the quiver is of type
A_{\infty}^{(0)}
, we assign labels in
\mathbb{Z}
to each vertex so that there is an edge
i\rightarrow j
if and only if
j=i-1
. We refer to the set of vertices in this quiver as
I
. Through the vertex labels,
I
is identified with
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
if
e\neq\infty
, or with
\mathbb{Z}
if
e=\infty
. The
quiver Hecke algebra

R_{n,e}
, or simply
R_{n}
, associated to the quiver above is defined with generators and relations. The description in algebraic notation can be found in many sources, see for instance
[
13
]
. We will give the generators and relations in diagrammatic notation, as we will be working mainly with diagrams. The following are the generators of
R_{n}
.

(2)

\begin{gathered}\psi_{r}=\hbox to114.6pt{\vbox to48.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-13.61044pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}

and

. The relations on this algebra include some natural isotopy relations (for instance,
\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\psi_{j}\psi_{i}
if
|i-j|\geq 2
, see
[

]
) which we do not describe in detail, along with the relations below. The only new results for
e=2
in this text appear in
section

4
, where we do not use the quiver Hecke algebra presentation, so we assume that
e>2
in the following relations for the sake of simplicity.

Finally, the
e(\boldsymbol{i})
form a set of mutually orthogonal idempotents.

(6)

\par e(\boldsymbol{i})e(\boldsymbol{j})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}}e(\boldsymbol{i}),\qquad\sum_{\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{n}}e(\boldsymbol{i})=1.

While the sum above is not finite if
e=\infty
, it does become finite in the cyclotomic quotient described below, as
e(\boldsymbol{i})=0
for all but finitely many values under this quotient.

Quiver Hecke algebras can be given a grading as follows.

(7)

\deg e(\boldsymbol{i})=0,\qquad\deg y_{r}=2,\qquad\deg\psi_{r}e(\boldsymbol{i})=-a_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}.

Considering
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
as a Coxeter group, let
w=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\dots s_{i_{m}}
be a word in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. That is, an element of the free group generated by the same Coxeter generators as the symmetric group. We use the notation
\psi_{w}=\psi_{i_{1}}\psi_{i_{2}}\dots\psi_{i_{m}}
.

To take a
cyclotomic quotient
we first fix a positive integer
\ell
(the level) and
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell})\in I^{\ell}
. We then have
(\Lambda(\kappa),\alpha_{i})=\#\{\kappa_{j}\mid\kappa_{j}=i\}
. We denote by
R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
the cyclotomic quotient of
R_{n,e}
with dominant weight
\Lambda=\Lambda(\kappa)
. It is the quotient of
R_{n,e}
by the relations
y_{1}^{(\Lambda,\alpha_{i_{1}})}e(\boldsymbol{i})=0
for all
\boldsymbol{i}\in I^{n}
.

It was shown by Brundan and Kleshchev
[
1
]
that, over a field, all integral cyclotomic Hecke algebras of type A are isomorphic to a cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra. Since quiver Hecke algebras are graded, this isomorphism endows cyclotomic Hecke algebras with a non-trivial graded structure. The parameter
e
is defined differently for the two algebras, but it is preserved under the isomorphism. It is called the
quantum characteristic
. Brundan and Kleshchev’s result justifies the notation
\mathcal{H}_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
for cyclotomic Hecke algebras, as it proves that all cyclotomic Hecke algebras over a field with parameters
n,e
and
\Lambda
are all isomorphic to the same cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra
R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
, and hence they are all isomorphic to each other.

Given a single diagram
u\in R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
, we assign positive integers to each string in increasing order from left to right at the bottom of the diagram as in
eq.

2
, so that every string is labeled by a number in
\{1,\dots,n\}
. When we refer to string
s
we mean the string labeled by the positive integer
s
. This labelling provides the set of strings with a total order, as strings can be compared through their integer labels.

2.4.
Specht modules

Graded lifts of Specht modules for
\mathcal{H}_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
were constructed by Brundan, Kleshchev and Wang
[
2
]
. Kleshchev, Mathas and Ram
[
12
]
gave a description of Specht modules in terms of a single generator and homogeneous relations.

The Specht module
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is generated by
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. The module
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is drawn by placing
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
at the top of an
R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
-diagram. We associate the top of the diagram with the nodes of the Young diagram of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
in order, according to the initial tableau
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. Each string is given a residue according to the residue of the node it reaches. An
i
-string
is a string reaching a node of residue
i
.

For each tableau
\mathfrak{t}
, there is a unique permutation
\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
. For each
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
, we fix a reduced word
w_{\mathfrak{t}}
for the permutation
\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
. This choice can be made in any number of ways. Given a tableau
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
, a
321
-pattern
is any triple of natural numbers
i<j<k
such that
\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(i)>\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(j)>\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(k)
. With an eye towards
6.1
, we choose
w_{\mathfrak{t}}
so that for each
321
-pattern in
\mathfrak{t}
where
\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(i),\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(j),\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(k)
have residues
i,i\pm 1,i
respectively, the diagram for
w_{\mathfrak{t}}
looks as follows when ignoring all other strings.

For
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
, we let
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\psi_{w_{\mathfrak{t}}}
. The elements
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
are well-defined up to a choice of reduced words
w_{\mathfrak{t}}
. In fact, it is easy to see that the choice we have made is enough to determine
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. In the diagrams for Specht modules, we will draw brown boxes at the top of the diagram around nodes which are in the same row of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. According to
[
12
]
, we need to quotient by the simple Specht relations

Define
\boldsymbol{i}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to be the sequence of residues
\left(\res\left((\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1}(1)\right),\dots,\res\left((\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1}(n)\right)\right)\in I^{n}
. We also need to quotient by the relation

(9)

v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}e(\boldsymbol{i})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}

and by the homogeneous Garnir relations, which are in general more complicated and significantly less intuitive in this graphical notation. However, in this text we will only need the simplest case, where

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad=\quad 0.

The Specht module
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
has a homogeneous basis given by
\{v_{\mathfrak{t}}\mid\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})\}

[
12
, Section 3.4]
, where
\deg(v_{\mathfrak{t}})=\deg(\mathfrak{t})
. We refer to elements
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
which are given by acting on the Specht generator
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
by a monomial in elements
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n-1},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
simply as
monomials
. Given a monomial
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, we write
\sigma_{v}
for the permutation that the strings in
v
form, and we let
\mathfrak{t}_{v}={\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\sigma_{v}
. Suppose
v
is given as
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\psi_{w}
, where
w
is a reduced word for a permutation
\sigma_{w}
such that
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\sigma_{w}
is standard. Then we say that
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is a
standard monomial
. In particular, the standard basis given by
\{v_{\mathfrak{t}}\mid\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\lambda}})\}
is composed of standard monomials.

Sometimes it is convenient to apply Specht relations only for some of the nodes of a diagram. For instance, consider the following diagram.

\dots
B
\dots
A

Here
A
and
B
represent some crossings between and some dots on the involved strings. Sometimes we want to focus on
B
, perform some operations on it by applying the appropriate relations, and keep
A
arbitrary. Continuously drawing
A
can be cumbersome, so we want to stop drawing this part of the diagram. Specht relations may be applied only to the strings coming out of
B
that do not enter
A
. We draw this as a
mixed
Specht module diagram.

\dots
B

We will use mixed diagrams like the above extensively throughout the text.

3.
Stubborn Strings

3.1.
Accessible Nodes

Stubborn strings were introduced in
[
13
]
. Lyle and Mathas observed the following: given a monomial
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, it is possible to find its decomposition in terms of basis elements by applying quiver Hecke and Specht relations
only in the direction where crossings are broken
. In this sense, no new crossings are created. Crossings are either preserved or undone after the application of a relation, but never created anew. Moreover,
i
and
j
-strings never switch places, so that an
i
-string can only reach
i
-nodes. We fix a multipartition
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for the rest of the section.

When a relation gives a summand where some
(i,i)
-crossing is no longer a crossing, we say that the relation
cuts
that crossing. For instance,
eqs.

5
and
4
cut crossings. We also say that a dot cuts a crossing in
eq.

5
and a string cuts a crossing in
eq.

4
. Since
(i,i)
-crossings may only be cut as in

\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to19.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.75 0 L 0 26.25}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\rightarrow\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to19.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{

and never as in
\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to19.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 26.25}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.75 0 L 0 26.25}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\rightarrow\hbox to12.38pt{\vbox to19.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 26.25 L 5.03 17.87 C 6.6 15.25 9.15 15.25 10.72 17.87 L 15.75 26.25}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.03 8.38 C 6.6 11 9.15 11 10.72 8.38 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
, we can infer a restriction on the nodes at the top of the diagram which a string may reach after cutting some
(i,i)
-crossings, as follows: start at the bottom of string
s
in the diagram for
v
and continuously move upward. At every
(i,i)
-crossing, make a choice to continue on the current string or move on to the other string in the crossing, and continue moving towards the top of the diagram. If we color all possible paths from the bottom of string
s
to the top of the diagram, we obtain a “tree diagram”. We call the set of nodes we can reach by this procedure the
accessible nodes
of string
s
in monomial
v
, and denote this set by
A_{v}(s)
. Let
Y_{n,e}^{\Lambda}\subset R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
be the
k
-subalgebra generated by
y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{V}_{v}
be the set of monomials which appear as summands inside
vY_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
by applying relations without creating any new crossings. In particular, for a monomial
v^{\prime}\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for which string
s
reaches a node
N\notin A_{v}(s)
, we have
v^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{V}_{v}
. Finally,
\mathcal{W}_{v}\subset\mathcal{V}_{v}
is the subset of standard monomials inside
\mathcal{V}_{v}
. We often omit the subscript in
A_{v}(s),\mathcal{V}_{v}
and
\mathcal{W}_{v}
when it is clear from the context.

Definition 3.1
.

We say a string
s
in a monomial
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is
stubborn
if it reaches the smallest node in
A(s)
. We say a string
s
is
co-stubborn
if it reaches the greatest node in
A(s)
.

Note that the definition above describes a weaker condition than the definition of stubborn strings in
[
13
]
.

Example 3.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=((1),(1),(1),(1),(1))
and
\kappa=(0,0,0,0,0)
. In the following diagram for an element
v\in S^{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
, we have labeled the nodes of
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
at the top of the diagram. Usually we label the residues of the nodes instead, but in this case it is not necessary, as all strings share the same residue.

(11)

N_{2}
N_{5}
N_{1}
N_{3}
s
N_{4}

We have drawn the Specht module diagram with thin black lines, and marked the top with labels
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4},N_{5}
for five nodes. We have drawn the tree diagram for string
s
with thicker green lines superposed over the Specht module diagram. We see that the tree reaches nodes
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4}
in green, and therefore
A(s)=\{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4}\}
. Since
s
reaches the largest node in
A(s)
, we see that
s
is co-stubborn.

Next we prove a few simple lemmas concerning stubborn strings. Recall that strings in a diagram are totally ordered through their integer labels.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a standard monomial, and let
s
be a string of residue
i
in
v
. Then
s
is stubborn if and only if all
i
-strings crossing
s
are smaller than
s
.

Proof.

Suppose
s
reaches node
N
in
v
. For
s
to be stubborn, all strings which cross
s
must reach a larger node than
N
. Since
v
is a standard monomial, strings cannot cross
s
twice in
v
. Therefore, only strings which are smaller than
s
may cross it. The converse is clear.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a standard monomial. Suppose
s
is a stubborn string in
v
reaching node
N
. Then for all
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
, the string reaching
N
is smaller than or equal to
s
.

Proof.

Consider
v\in S^{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
as an element of
R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
. There is an involution
\omega:R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}
that performs a reflection across a horizontal line through a diagram. It is clear from the previous lemma that string
s
in
v\in R_{n,e}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is stubborn if and only if its associated string
\omega(s)
in
\omega(v)
is co-stubborn. Therefore, for any
\omega(v^{\prime})
with
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
,
\omega(s)
may only reach an element at the top of the diagram to the left of the element that it reaches in
\omega(v)
. Consequently, for any
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
, only a string that is smaller than or equal to
s
may reach node
N
.
∎

The following lemma concerns standard monomials whose underlying permutation is fully commutative (that is,
321
-avoiding). We refer to these as
fully commutative standard monomials
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a fully commutative standard monomial. Then all strings in
v
are either stubborn or co-stubborn.

Proof.

Suppose that string
s
in the diagram for
v
is neither stubborn nor co-stubborn. Then there must be two strings crossing it from opposite directions: a string
s_{1}
smaller than
s
crosses it from bottom-left to top-right, and a string
s_{2}
greater than
s
crosses it from bottom-right to top-left. Since
v
is a standard monomial, strings may not intersect twice. Therefore,
s_{2},s,s_{1}
make a
321
-pattern, which is in contradiction with the assumption that the underlying permutation for
v
is fully commutative.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a standard monomial. Suppose
s
is a stubborn string with residue
i
in
v
and reaches node
N\in[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
. Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
be any diagram where at least one of the
(i,i)
-crossings of
s
has been cut. Then
N\notin A_{v^{\prime}}(s)
.

Proof.

By
3.3
, only smaller strings than
s
may cross
s
in
v
. If they do, then they must cross
s
only once from bottom-left to top-right, as
v
is a standard monomial. To reach
N
, string
s
must travel a path through its tree diagram to reach
N
.

Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
. At each crossing of its own tree diagram,
s
may continue to the left or right, depending on whether the crossing has been cut to obtain diagram
v^{\prime}
from diagram
v
. At the lowest
(i,i)
-crossing of
s
in
v
which has been cut,
s
veers to the right in the diagram for
v^{\prime}
. It is impossible for string
s
to return to its original course in
v
, as it is situated to the right-hand side of the path that
s
takes in
v
, and no
i
-string intersects
s
from bottom-right to upper-left in
v
.
∎

The following lemma is clear from the definition of stubborn strings.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial, and suppose a string
s
in the diagram for
v
is stubborn. Then
v=\sum c_{\mathfrak{t}}v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, where the sum is over all standard
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
-tableaux and
c_{\mathfrak{t}}\neq 0
only if
\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(s)
is a node larger than or equal to
\mathfrak{t}_{v}^{-1}(s)
.

The next two definitions describe stronger conditions than stubbornness.

Definition 3.8
.

We say a string
s
in a monomial
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is
strongly (co-)stubborn
if
s
is (co-)stubborn in all
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
.

Definition 3.9
.

We say that a string
s
of residue
i
in monomial
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is
immobile
if, for any monomial
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
where an
(i,i)
-crossing of string
s
has been cut, we have
v^{\prime}=0
. We say that a node is immobile if the string reaching it is immobile.

In particular, strings which do not cross other strings with the same residue are immobile, as the condition above is vacuously true.

Given a subset
\mathfrak{A}
of the strings of a particular residue in a monomial
v
, we will need a definition for
A(\mathfrak{A})
, the accessible sets of nodes for strings in
\mathfrak{A}
. We start with an example.

Example 3.10
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be the diagram below, where
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=((1),(1),(1),(1),(1))
and all strings share the same residue.

(12)

s_{1}
N_{2}
s_{2}
N_{4}
s_{3}
N_{5}
s_{4}
N_{1}
s_{5}
N_{3}

Then strings
s_{1},s_{2}
and
s_{3}
are all co-stubborn, while
s_{4}
and
s_{5}
are stubborn. Moreover,
s_{1}
is strongly co-stubborn, while
s_{5}
is strongly stubborn.

Next we look at the accessible nodes of
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
. We have
A(s_{2})=\{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4}\}
and
A(s_{3})=\{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4},N_{5}\}
, but note that
\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
can never reach the set
\{N_{1},N_{2}\}
in a diagram in
\mathcal{V}_{v}
. To explain the reason, we draw a thin red line through the middle of the diagram.

(13)

N_{2}
s_{2}
N_{4}
s_{3}
N_{5}
N_{1}
N_{3}

Consider the graph with vertices given by all
(i,i)
-crossings, all origins of
i
-strings at the bottom of the diagram, and all
i
-nodes at the top of the diagram; and edges given by the segments of
i
-strings connecting the vertices in the diagram above. Designate the starting points of strings
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
as sources and nodes
N_{1},N_{2}
as sinks. Then it is possible to cut off the sources from the sinks by cutting a single edge. By the max-flow/min-cut theorem, the maximal flow from
s_{2},s_{3}
to
N_{1},N_{2}
is at most
1
, so that only one of
s_{2}
or
s_{3}
may reach nodes
N_{1},N_{2}
in a single diagram. Therefore, it makes sense to define
A(\{s_{2},s_{3}\})
in such a way that
\{N_{1},N_{2}\}\notin A(\{s_{2},s_{3}\})
.

The argument above motivates the following definition and can easily be modified to prove the lemma below it.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial,
\mathfrak{A}
be a set of
m
strings in
v
with the same residue. Define the graph of residue
i
associated with the diagram of
v
as in the example above. Then
A(\mathfrak{A})
is the set of all
\mathcal{N}\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
of size
m
such that the maximal flow from the start of the strings of
\mathfrak{A}
to the nodes in
\mathcal{N}
in diagram
v
is
m
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial and let
\mathfrak{A}
be a set of strings in
v
with the same residue. Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
, and let
\mathcal{N}
be the set of nodes reached by strings
\mathfrak{A}
in
v^{\prime}
. Then
\mathcal{N}\in A(\mathfrak{A})
.

We introduce a partial order between sets of nodes of the same size. Let
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
be two sets of nodes such that
|\mathcal{N}|=|\mathcal{M}|
. Then we write
\mathcal{N}\succ\mathcal{M}
if the greatest node of
\mathcal{N}
is greater than the greatest node of
\mathcal{M}
.

We define two notions of stubbornness for sets of strings.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial. Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a set of strings with residue
i
reaching the set of nodes
\mathcal{N}
in
v
. Then we say that
\mathfrak{A}
is
stubborn
if
\mathcal{N}
is minimal in
A(\mathfrak{A})
. We say that
\mathfrak{A}
is
strongly stubborn
if
\mathfrak{A}
is stubborn for all
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
.

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.63092pt}{54.40465pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Write
\mathcal{L}=\{l_{1},\dots,l_{n}\}
and
\mathcal{R}=\{r_{1},\dots,r_{m}\}
. Note that all strings in
\mathcal{L}
are co-stubborn and all strings in
\mathcal{R}
are stubborn. Then we claim that
\mathcal{R}
is strongly stubborn.

Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
. Let
l_{x}
be the rightmost string in
\mathcal{L}
such that at least one of its crossings in
v
has been cut to reach diagram
v^{\prime}
. Then we argue that the rightmost node reached by a string in
\mathcal{R}
is
L_{x}
. Indeed, after we cut some crossing for string
l_{x}
,
L_{x}
is no longer in
A(l_{x})
. Therefore, some other string
s
must reach node
L_{x}
in
v^{\prime}
. All strings
l_{y}>l_{x}
reach
L_{y}
, so we must show that
s\in\mathcal{R}
. If we assume the contrary, then
s\in\mathcal{L}
, and moreover
s>l_{x}
because all the strings in
\mathcal{L}
are co-stubborn. But this leads to a contradiction: by assumption, we have not cut any crossings for strings larger than
l_{x}
, so any string larger than
l_{x}
cannot reach
L_{x}
in
v^{\prime}
.

In a further diagram
v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v^{\prime}}
, the rightmost node reached by a string in
\mathcal{R}
is either
L_{x}
or a node
L_{y}>L_{x}
, where
l_{y}
is the rightmost string in
\mathcal{L}
such that at least one of its crossings in
v^{\prime}
has been cut to reach diagram
v^{\prime\prime}
. We conclude that
\mathcal{R}
is strongly stubborn.

The argument above can be used to prove the following more general lemma.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a standard monomial. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of strings in
v
, and denote the rest of the strings in the diagram by
\mathcal{L}
. Suppose any two strings
r\in\mathcal{R}
and
l\in\mathcal{L}
may only intersect if
r>l
. Suppose further that the strings in
\mathcal{L}
do not intersect each other. Then
\mathcal{R}
is strongly stubborn.

Stubborn sets of strings satisfy the following property, which generalizes
3.6
and can be proved in a similar manner.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a standard monomial. Suppose
\mathfrak{A}
is a stubborn set of
i
-strings in
v
reaching the set of nodes
\mathcal{N}\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
. Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
be any diagram where at least one of the
(i,i)
-crossings of a string
s\in\mathfrak{A}
with a string
s^{\prime}\notin\mathfrak{A}
has been cut. Then
\mathcal{N}\notin A_{v^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{A})
.

3.2.
String activity

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial, and suppose
s
is a string in
v
with residue
i
such that all other strings have residue
j
with
|i-j|\geq 2
. We refer to such strings as
inert
strings. Then
eqs.

3
,
4
and
5
simplify to

Since all relations involving
s
are invariant when the string disappears, we may simply stop drawing
s
. The extra terms in
eq.

4
and
eq.

5
which do not appear for inert strings are called
error terms
.

The following lemma is clear from the definitions.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial, and suppose
s
is an immobile string in
v
with residue
i
. Then the relations

\hbox to33.91pt{\vbox to61.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-11.47156pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 31.5 47.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.00143pt}{37.97578pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

hold for string
s
.

In this sense, immobile strings are partially inactive: dots and strings can be pulled through their crossings without leaving error terms. On the other hand, they differ from inert strings, as
eq.

3
is not simplified, and sliding immobile strings past crossings may leave error terms.

Sometimes we want to show that a certain monomial is non-zero, but carrying out a full decomposition into basis elements is computationally challenging. If the monomial features stubborn or co-stubborn strings, then the next lemma can be helpful.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be a monomial, and suppose
s
is a (co-)stubborn string in
v
. Substitute the usual relations involving
s
for the relations in
3.17
, and compute a “simplified” decomposition of
v
into basis elements by successively applying the usual quiver Hecke and Specht relations, except for this substitution. If the result is non-zero, then the actual decomposition of
v
into basis elements is also non-zero. Moreover, if a basis element appears in the simplified decomposition, then it also appears in the actual decomposition with the same coefficient.

Proof.

Suppose string
s
reaches node
N
in
v
. Let us compute the terms in the decomposition of
v
that feature string
s
reaching node
N
. Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
be any monomial in
\mathcal{V}
where string
s
has been cut at least once. Then
N\notin A_{v^{\prime}}(s)
. Therefore, no monomial where
s
reaches
N
can be obtained from
v^{\prime}
, and we may discard
v^{\prime}
in our current computation.

On the other hand, by substituting the usual relations by those in
3.17
we are discarding exactly all terms
v^{\prime}
where string
s
has been cut. It follows that we will obtain all terms in the decomposition of
v
that feature string
s
reaching node
N
. The consequences in the statement follow easily.
∎

If we apply the above lemma to a string, we may also say that we
immobilize
that string. In computations, we draw both immobile and immobilized strings with green dashed lines. As long as we do not slide strings over green dashed lines, creating double crossings, and we do not slide crossings past green dashed lines, these lines can be ignored. If several green dashed lines are next to each other, we may bundle them together and draw them as a single line.

Example 3.19
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be the diagram below, where
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=((1),(1),(1),(1),(1),(1))
and all strings have the same residue.

(14)

Since the fourth and fifth strings are stubborn, we may immobilize them, and since they are right next to each other we draw them as a single string.

(15)

Next, we may slide the green dot up through the third string. The resulting diagram when the dot reaches the top of the string equals zero due to
eq.

8
, so the above simplifies to

(16)

\lxSVG@closescope {{

By
3.18
, this term is guaranteed to appear in the full decomposition of the original diagram with the same multiplicity.

We are especially interested in the case where
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is a fully commutative standard monomial. Then all strings of
v
are stubborn or co-stubborn by
3.5
. Let
s
be one such string. By the fully commutative condition, no string can cross
s
twice in any monomial
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
where the crossings of
s
have not been cut. In particular, the following holds.

Lemma 3.20
.

Suppose
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is a fully commutative standard monomial. Let
s
be an immobile or immobilized string in
v
. Then,
s
does not intersect any string more than once in any non-zero monomial
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{v}
.

In this case, there is no danger of creating double crossings when applying the relations in
3.17
to immobile or immobilized strings. Consequently, we may stop drawing those strings and perform our computations without considering them.

4.
Change of rings

Let
r
be a prime number,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
e=r^{a}
and let
\zeta
be a primitive
e
-th root of unity. Let
M,N
be
H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])
-modules. Then
M_{\mathbb{C}}\coloneq\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta]}M
is an
H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
-module, and according to
[
5
, Theorem 2.38]
, we have

(17)

\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})}(M_{\mathbb{C}},N_{\mathbb{C}})\cong\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta]}\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N).

In particular, it is clear that
\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})}(M_{\mathbb{C}},N_{\mathbb{C}})\neq 0
if and only if
\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)\neq 0
.

Recall that the cyclotomic polynomial
\Phi_{e}(x)
satisfies
\Phi_{e}(1)=r
, so that the map
\phi:\mathbb{Z}[\zeta]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{r}
defined by
\phi(\zeta)=1
is a homomorphism of rings with kernel
\mathfrak{p}=(1-\zeta)
.

Proposition 4.1
.

There exists a functor
\mathcal{F}:H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])\text{-}\mmod\rightarrow H_{n,1}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{F}_{r})\text{-}\mmod
defined as
M\mapsto M/\mathfrak{p}M
, and
\mathcal{F}(\psi)\neq 0
if and only if
\psi\in\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)\setminus\mathfrak{p}\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)
. In particular, if
\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)\neq\{0\}
then it follows that
\Hom_{H_{n,1}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{F}_{r})}(\mathcal{F}(M),\mathcal{F}(N))\neq\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
\Psi
be the matrix associated with
\psi
, considered as a linear transformation. It is clear that
\phi({\Psi})
, the matrix obtained by applying
\phi
to each entry of
\Psi
, represents a homomorphism from
M/\mathfrak{p}M
to
N/\mathfrak{p}N
, which confirms that
\mathcal{F}
is a functor.

It is clear that, if
\psi\in\mathfrak{p}\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)
, then
\mathcal{F}(\psi)=0
. Now, suppose that
\mathcal{F}(\psi)=0
, so that
\phi(\Psi)=0
. We show that then
1-\zeta
divides all entries of
\Psi
. Let
x\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta]
be one such entry, so that
\phi(x)=0
. Writing
x=\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}\zeta^{i}
, it follows that
\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}
is a multiple of
r
. But we also have
x=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}(\zeta^{i}-1)\right)
. The number
1-\zeta
clearly divides each summand in the second sum, and it divides the first sum by
[
5
, 4.38]
. Therefore
1-\zeta
divides
x
, and we see that
\psi\in\mathfrak{p}\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])}(M,N)
.
∎

We write
M_{\mathbb{F}_{r}}\coloneq\mathcal{F}(M)=M/\mathfrak{p}M
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
M,N
be
H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta])
-modules and suppose that
\Hom_{H_{n,\zeta}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})}(M_{\mathbb{C}},N_{\mathbb{C}})\neq\{0\}
. Then
\Hom_{H_{n,1}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{F}_{r})}(M_{\mathbb{F}_{r}},N_{\mathbb{F}_{r}})\neq\{0\}
.

The Brundan–Kleshchev isomorphism yields an analogous result for Specht modules of cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
r
be a prime number,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
e=r^{a}
. Let
M,N
be
R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
-modules and suppose that
\Hom_{R_{n,e}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})}(M,N)\neq\{0\}
. Then
\Hom_{R_{n,r}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{F}_{r})}(M_{\mathbb{F}_{r}},N_{\mathbb{F}_{r}})\neq\{0\}
.

Informally, let
p
be the characteristic of the field and
e
be the length of the quiver
A_{e-1}^{(1)}
corresponding to a cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra. Then, if we have an
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
-module homomorphism under
(p,e)=(0,r^{a})
for some integer
a
, it follows that we have an
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
-module homomorphism under
(p,e)=(r,r)
.

Example 4.4
.

Let
R_{5,4}^{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathbb{C})
be the cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra with parameters
n=5,e=4,p=0
. The main result in
[
13
]
gives a non-zero homomorphism from
S^{\lambda}
to
S^{\mu}
, where
\lambda=(3,2),\mu=(5)
. This is because the two shapes differ by a single horizontal strip of length less than
e=4
with matching residues:

Then an application of
4.3
shows that there is a non-zero homomorphism in
\Hom_{R_{5,2}^{\Lambda_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}(S^{\lambda}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}},S^{\mu}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}})
, which cannot be directly deduced from the main result in
[
13
]
. The existence of a homomorphism can also be seen by applying
1.1
.

In fact, the application of
4.3
to the main theorem in
[
13
]
yields a full generalization of the classical Carter–Payne theorem for symmetric groups (
1.1
). We do not write the details because our main theorem is a further generalization.

A result analogous to
4.2
for decomposition matrices can be found in
[
15
, Section 6.2]
. In the above discussion we did not make use of the generators and relations of the cyclotomic Hecke algebra, so the result can be made more general. For instance, the cyclotomic
q
-Schur algebra may be considered instead of the cyclotomic Hecke algebra.

Remark
.

Our proof of
4.3
used the Hecke algebra setting. A proof in the
quiver
Hecke algebra setting is highly desirable for several reasons. Firstly, the degree of a homomorphism is in general not preserved under
4.3
, unless
a=1
. For instance, in
4.4
, the homomorphism where
e=4,p=0
has degree
+1
, as the description of the degree in
[
13
]
shows. However, the homomorphism where
e=2,p=2
has degree
0
. Secondly, it is likely that a finer result holds for quiver Hecke algebras, where a homomorphism between
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
-modules under
(p,e)=(r,r^{a})
implies the existence of a homomorphism of
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
-modules under
(p,e)=(r,r^{a-1})
. However, Hecke algebras do not exist for
(p,e)=(r,r^{a})
unless
a=1
, so the finer result cannot be obtained via the Brundan–Kleshchev isomorphism. A proof of
4.3
directly in the quiver Hecke algebra setting is likely to shine light on both of these phenomena.

5.
One-row Carter–Payne homomorphisms

If
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\vdash n
and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vdash n+\gamma
are multipartitions such that
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\subset[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]
, define
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
to be the unique maximal tableau in the dominance order such that
(\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}})_{\downarrow n}=\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. A
row-strip

[\xi]\subset[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
is a contiguous set of horizontally-adjacent nodes inside a component of
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
. It is said to be
removable
if
[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\setminus[\xi]
is the shape of a partition.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
be multipartitions of
n
and suppose that

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\vartriangleright{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, that
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is a multipartition of

n+\gamma
such that
[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]=[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\cup[{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
, and
[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]\backslash[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
and
[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]\backslash[{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
are both
removable row-strips of the same length
\gamma<e
and with the same sequence of residues
J=\res({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})=\res({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
. Write
[\lambda^{*}]=[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]\backslash[{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
and
[\mu^{*}]=[{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]\backslash[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
, and label the maximal removable row-strips which are subshapes of
[\mu^{*}]
between
[\mu^{*}]
and
[\lambda^{*}]
as
[\mu^{*}]=[\xi^{0}]\prec[\xi^{1}]\prec\dots\prec[\xi^{c-1}]\prec[\xi^{c}]=[\lambda^{*}]
. That is, within the set of removable row-strips which are subshapes of
[\mu^{*}]
, we consider only those which are maximal under set containment, so that we do not take
[\xi]
if there exists
[\rho]
with
[\xi]\subset[\rho]
(see
5.2
). We assume throughout the section that
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
is defined over a field of characteristic
p
, possibly zero, and with respect to a quiver
A_{e-1}^{(1)}
with
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

(
[
13
, Theorem 3.12]
)

Under the conditions above, there exists a homomorphism
\theta\in\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\langle a-b+2c\rangle,S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, where

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\#\{N\in\Add({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\res(N)\in J\text{ and }\row({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})<\row(N)\leq\row({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\},

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\#\{N\in\Rem({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\res(N)\in J\text{ and }\row({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})<\row(N)\leq\row({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\backslash{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\}.

Moreover,
a-b+2c>0
and
\theta(v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}})
is a basis element of
S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(for the basis described in
section

2.4
).

We define
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\mu}})
so that
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
is the basis element described in the theorem. This tableau is equal to the tableau
\mathfrak{t}_{z+1}
in
[
13
, Section 3]
, but we construct it slightly differently.

Let
j\in\{1,\dots,c\}
, and suppose that
\xi^{j}
is a row-strip of length
l\leq\gamma
. Let
\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}
be the permutation that exchanges the numbers appearing in
[\xi^{j}]
in the tableau
\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
with the numbers
\{n+1,\dots,n+l\}
, in order. Then we have
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}=\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{1}}\dots\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{c-1}}\sigma_{\mu^{*},\lambda^{*}}\right)_{\downarrow n}
. Recall that permutations act on tableaux by permuting the entries of the tableaux.

Example 5.2
.

Let
p=0
,
e=4
and
\Lambda=(0,2,1)
. Suppose
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=(\varnothing,(3,2,2),(4,3))\vdash 14
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=((3),(3,2,2),(4))\vdash 14
, so that
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=((3),(3,2,2),(4,3))\vdash 17
. We draw
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with the residues corresponding to each node.

and

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\right)\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\mu}}),

where we have shaded in the shapes
[\mu^{*}]=[\xi^{0}]\prec\dots\prec[\xi^{4}]=[\lambda^{*}]
. Entries of
[\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}]
outside every
[\xi^{j}]
are fixed under the actions of the permutations
\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}
. Next we apply the permutations
\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}
in order. As an example, we have

\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{1}}=\left(\,\hbox to37.19pt{\vbox to13.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-5.29047pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

Finally,
4.3
shows that if we set
e=p=2
, there is another homomorphism
\overline{\theta}\in\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
. Computing this homomorphism in the cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra setting requires using the Brundan–Kleshchev isomorphism twice to switch between the cyclotomic Hecke algebra and the cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra settings, which is a lengthy computation. The homomorphism space can instead be computed directly, and in this case we find that there is a homomorphism of degree 7 which sends
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}}
to
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\mu}})
is defined as

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\left(\,\hbox to37.19pt{\vbox to13.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-5.29047pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

Here we have elected to shade the nodes
N
such that
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}(N)\neq\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(N)
. In this case
\overline{\theta}
sends the Specht generator
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}}
to a monomial, but this does not happen in general, even if
\theta
does send the Specht generator to a monomial.

The proof of
5.1
we give here differs slightly from the one given in
[
13
]
. On the one hand, this will make it possible to prove the explicit description of the homomorphisms given above, which is a new result. On the other hand, the proof here resembles more closely the proof of the more general statement in the next section.

Recall our definitions for
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
at the beginning of the section and let
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
be either
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
or
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. Let
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
be a standard tableau such that
(\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})_{\downarrow n}=\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. The following notation is borrowed from
[
13
]
.

\displaystyle\Std_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\gamma}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{s}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\mathfrak{s}^{-1}(n+g)=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(n+g)\text{ for }1\leq g\leq\gamma\},

\displaystyle\Std^{\vee}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{s}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\Shape(\mathfrak{s}_{\downarrow n})\vartriangleright{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\},

\displaystyle\Std^{\wedge}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{s}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\Shape(\mathfrak{s}_{\downarrow n})\not\trianglelefteq{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\},

\displaystyle\Std_{n}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\mathfrak{t}_{\downarrow n}=\mathfrak{t}^{\Shape(\mathfrak{t}_{\downarrow n})}\},

\displaystyle\Std^{e}_{\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{t}\in\Std_{n}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mid\res_{\mathfrak{t}}(n+g)=\res_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(n+g),\text{ for }1\leq g\leq\gamma\},

and

\displaystyle\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\mathfrak{s}}\mid\mathfrak{s}\in\Std_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}^{\gamma}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\cup\Std^{\vee}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\mathfrak{s}}\mid\mathfrak{s}\in\Std^{\vee}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\hat{S}^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\mathfrak{s}}\mid\mathfrak{s}\in\Std_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}^{\gamma}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\cup\Std^{\wedge}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\hat{S}^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\mathfrak{s}}\mid\mathfrak{s}\in\Std^{\wedge}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Embed
R_{n}
in
R_{n+\gamma}
in the natural way, so that
v\in R_{n}
is mapped to the image of
v\otimes 1
under the map
R_{n}\otimes R_{\gamma}\hookrightarrow R_{n+\gamma}
. Informally,
R_{n}
acts on the first
n
strings of
R_{n+\gamma}
. Moreover, the embedding descends to the cyclotomic quotients
R_{n}^{\Lambda}\subset R_{n+\gamma}^{\Lambda}
. To prove
5.1
we will need the following lemma from
[
13
]
, based on work by Ellers and Murray
[
8
]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

(
[
13
, Lemma 2.24]
)

For
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}_{n+\gamma}^{\ell}
and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\ell}
with
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\subset{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, let
\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}},\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})

be standard tableaux such that
(\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}})_{\downarrow n}=\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
,

(\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})_{\downarrow n}=\mathfrak{t}^{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\vartriangleright\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
. Suppose that there
exists a homogeneous element
L\in R_{n+\gamma}^{\Lambda}
such that
L
commutes with
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
and

\textbf{(A)}\quad\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}}L\subseteq\hat{S}^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}},\quad\textbf{(B)}\quad\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}L\subseteq\hat{S}^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\quad\text{and}\quad\textbf{(C)}\quad v_{\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}}L\notin\hat{S}^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}.

Then
\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\langle d\rangle,S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\neq 0
, where

d=(\deg\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}-\deg\mathfrak{t}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})-(\deg\mathfrak{t}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}-\deg\mathfrak{t}^{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})+\deg L
.

Remark
.

In this text we use
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
as the tableaux
\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the lemma.

From now on we label the residues in
[\mu^{*}]
and
[\lambda^{*}]
as
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\gamma}
, so that
i_{j}=i_{j-1}+1\text{ (mod $e$)}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
v\in S^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
be a standard monomial such that
\mathfrak{t}_{v}\in\Std_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{e}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
. Suppose that string
s
of residue
i_{1}
reaches a node
N
which is not in a shape
[\xi^{j}]
for any
j\in\{0,\dots,c\}
. Then
s
is an immobile string.

Proof.

Draw a diagram of all
i_{1}
-strings in
v
. The condition
\mathfrak{t}_{v}\in\Std_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{e}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
implies that when we draw only the strings of residue
i_{1}
of the diagram for
v
we obtain

(20)

\hbox to106.49pt{\vbox to86.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-15.95401pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces;
;
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 78.74}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Consider only the upper part of the diagram, so that the lowest crossing is the one between
s
and
n+1
. Then if the crossing is broken, string
n+1
will be clearly immobile (as it does not intersect other strings in this part of the diagram) and reach node
N
. But
N
is not inside a shape of
[\xi^{j}]
for any
j
, so that no tableau with
n+1
in node
N
can be standard. The diagram must then equal zero. It is clear that if the upper part of the diagram equals zero, then the whole diagram must equal zero. It follows that breaking the
(i_{1},i_{1})
-crossing of string
s
always gives zero, or in other words,
s
is immobile.
∎

The following lemma is the main computational tool in the proof of
5.1
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
k<e
. Let
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in I
be residues so that
i_{j}=i_{j-1}+1

(mod
e
)
. Then

If
s=1
, there is nothing to prove. We follow with the induction step. Applying a braid relation at the red circle gives a sum of two diagrams. In one of the diagrams, the
i_{s-1}
-string pulls past the
(i_{s},i_{s})
-crossing. Then the
i_{s-1}
-string can be pulled past every further crossing of the co-stubborn
i_{s+1}
-string, where
eq.

8
shows that this diagram equals zero. It follows that the diagram above equals

(22)

i_{2}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
i_{s-1}
i_{s}
i_{s}
i_{s-1}
i_{s+1}
i_{s+1}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
i_{k}
i_{k}
i_{1}
i_{2}

Since
k<e
, the residues
i_{s}
and
i_{t}
are not adjacent in the quiver
\Gamma
for any
t\in{1,\dots,s-2}
. Therefore, we can use
eq.

3
to slide the
i_{s}
-string to the left through the strings with residues
i_{s-2},\dots,i_{1}
. Induction is then complete. Taking
s=k
proves the lemma.
∎

Following the same convention as
[
13
]
, we define
e_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sum_{j\in I^{n}}e(j_{1},\dots,j_{n},i_{1},\dots,i_{\gamma})
, where
i_{h}=\res_{t_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(n+h)
. This corresponds to the image of
e(i_{1},\dots,i_{\gamma})
under
R_{\gamma}\hookrightarrow R_{n+\gamma}\twoheadrightarrow R_{n+\gamma}^{\Lambda}
. Letting
R_{n}^{\Lambda}\subset R_{n+\gamma}^{\Lambda}
as above,
e_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
commutes with
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
.

Proof of
5.1
.

We will prove the theorem by applying
5.3
. We argue that
L=y_{n+1}^{c}e_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
satisfies all three conditions of the lemma.

First we will check conditions
(A)
and
(B)
of
5.3
. Let
v_{\mathfrak{t}}\in S^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
be the basis element of
S^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
corresponding to tableau
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
. Since
L
commutes with the natural inclusions of
R_{n}
and
R_{\gamma}
into
R_{n+\gamma}
, we may assume that
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std_{n}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
. Since
L
includes the idempotent
e^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, we can further assume that
\mathfrak{t}\in\Std_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{e}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
, as
v_{\mathfrak{t}}L=0
otherwise.

Next, draw only the
i_{1}
-strings in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
that reach nodes inside a subshape of
[\mu^{*}]
. Note that there may exist subshapes of
[\mu^{*}]
different from
[\xi^{0}],\dots,[\xi^{c}]
. Due to
5.4
, the
i_{1}
-strings that we do not draw are immobile.

(23)

\cdots
\cdots
N
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
n+1
\underbrace{\hskip 28.45274pt}_{k}

Here
N\coloneq(\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}})^{-1}(n+1)
, and we define
k
, as in the figure, to be the number of strings crossing string
n+1
which reach a node
N^{\prime}\leq N
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
and which are not immobile according to
5.4
. Clearly, for
v_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
we have
k<c
and for
v_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\check{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
we have
k\leq c
. In particular, for
v_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
we have
k=c
.

Note that string
n+1
is strongly stubborn, so it must reach accessible nodes that are further and further to the right as its crossings are cut. If
k<c
then for all non-zero summands in the right-hand side of
v_{\mathfrak{t}}L=v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{n+1}^{c}e_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sum c_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
, node
N^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})^{-1}(n+1)
must be to the right of
N
, and therefore
\Shape(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})_{\downarrow n}\not\trianglelefteq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. This proves that condition
(B)
of
5.3
holds. If
k=c
, then for all non-zero summands in the right-hand side of
v_{\mathfrak{t}}L=\sum c_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
, node
N^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})^{-1}(n+1)
is to the right of or equal to
N
. If
N^{\prime}
is to the right of
N
then
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\in\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. If
N=N^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, then strings
n+1,\dots,n+\gamma
must all reach a node in the same row as
N
and to the right of
N
due to standardness. Indeed,
N
is the leftmost node of
[\lambda^{*}]
, so all the other nodes in
[\lambda^{*}]
must be reached by the strings
n+2,\dots,n+\gamma
. In this case,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\Std^{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
, so that
v_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\in\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
. This proves that condition
(A)
of
5.3
holds.

Finally we discuss condition
(C)
. We will show that
v_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}L\notin\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. For
v_{\mathfrak{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
we have
k=c
, so all ways to cut the crossings of string
n+1
in diagram
eq.

23
are in
\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
except the case where we cut the uppermost
c
intersections. We show that this summand is not in
\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, and this implies that condition
(C)
of
5.3
is satisfied because it is the only summand not in
\hat{S}^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

We first consider the outcome of cutting a single
(i_{1},i_{1})
-crossing where string
n+1
meets with a string reaching the first node in
[\xi^{j}]
, where
j\in[c]
. Suppose that
[\xi^{j}]
has
l
nodes. Then the situation after cutting the crossing is as follows.

We apply
5.5
to the area surrounded by the red polygon. We can do this despite the strings to the right of the red box, because their residues are all different from the residues of the strings in the red box. (Alternatively, we can apply
6.5
.) We obtain

This process can be repeated for each of the
c
crossings between string
n+1
and a string of residue
i_{1}
reaching a node accommodating
n+1
. For a bipartition
(\mu^{*},\xi^{j})
made out of two rows, let
\mathfrak{t}_{\xi^{j},\mu^{*}}
be the unique tableau such that
(\mathfrak{t}_{\xi^{j},\mu^{*}})_{\downarrow l}=(0,\mathfrak{t}^{\xi^{j}})
. Then, note that
\sigma^{\prime}_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}
is the unique permutation such that
\mathfrak{t}^{(\mu^{*},\xi^{j})}\sigma^{\prime}_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\xi^{j},\mu^{*}}\sigma_{\mu^{*},\xi^{j}}
, where
\mathfrak{t}^{(\mu^{*},\xi^{j})}
is the initial tableau as usual. In this way, if we do not draw immobile strings, we reach a diagram like the following.

v^{\prime}=\hbox to155.78pt{\vbox to158.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 106.34267pt\lower-7.83301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces;
;
;
;
